I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.

I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. My husband will sit next to me and I know I should feel… comforted, I guess, or happy that he’s there.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of what made me come in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine presence of significant weight loss or gain", "question": "Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?"}

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.

But it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.

That’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. My husband will sit next to me and I know I should feel… comforted, I guess, or happy that he’s there.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of what made me come in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so?     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. My husband will sit next to me and I know I should feel… comforted, I guess, or happy that he’s there.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of what made me come in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. Not even anything special, you know, just that normal end-of-the-day feeling.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.\n\nThat’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean… some days I feel low, but then I get distracted by work stuff or what needs to be done at home. Like yesterday I was trying to remember if we were out of laundry detergent, and then I ended up cleaning under the sink for no real reason.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. I’m… I don’t know. “Depressed” feels like such a big word, and I keep trying to compare it to what I think depressed is supposed to look like.

Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. He’s actually been pretty patient. Sometimes too patient, almost, which makes me feel weird… like when someone keeps asking if you want tea and you just want them to stop watching you. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. My husband will sit next to me and I know I should feel… comforted, I guess, or happy that he’s there.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of what made me come in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. And I still turn them on, you know, because that’s what I always did.\n\nBut I don’t really feel pleasure from it. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.\n\nThat’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean… some days I feel low, but then I get distracted by work stuff or what needs to be done at home. Like yesterday I was trying to remember if we were out of laundry detergent, and then I ended up cleaning under the sink for no real reason.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. Not even anything special, you know, just that normal end-of-the-day feeling.\n\nAnd now I’ll still sit there, the TV’s on, he’s there, and I’m thinking about stupid things like whether I forgot to move boxes at work or if the freezer door got left open—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. It’s just… I know I should feel comfort, or a little happy, or something. But there’s just nothing there.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt depressed most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… I don’t know. “Depressed” feels like such a big word, and I keep trying to compare it to what I think depressed is supposed to look like.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and I’m still doing things, still moving boxes, still answering people if they ask where something is. And then I get home and I’m tired, but I mean, I’m always tired after a shift, you know? So I don’t know if that counts as depressed most of the day.\n\nI guess I’ve felt off a lot, but saying most of the day for most days… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. My husband will sit next to me and I know I should feel… comforted, I guess, or happy that he’s there.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of what made me come in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. And I still turn them on, you know, because that’s what I always did.\n\nBut I don’t really feel pleasure from it. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.\n\nThat’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean… some days I feel low, but then I get distracted by work stuff or what needs to be done at home. Like yesterday I was trying to remember if we were out of laundry detergent, and then I ended up cleaning under the sink for no real reason.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. Not even anything special, you know, just that normal end-of-the-day feeling.\n\nAnd now I’ll still sit there, the TV’s on, he’s there, and I’m thinking about stupid things like whether I forgot to move boxes at work or if the freezer door got left open—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. It’s just… I know I should feel comfort, or a little happy, or something. But there’s just nothing there.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt depressed most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… I don’t know. “Depressed” feels like such a big word, and I keep trying to compare it to what I think depressed is supposed to look like.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and I’m still doing things, still moving boxes, still answering people if they ask where something is. And then I get home and I’m tired, but I mean, I’m always tired after a shift, you know? So I don’t know if that counts as depressed most of the day.\n\nI guess I’ve felt off a lot, but saying most of the day for most days… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s been about three weeks now, since that pallet collapsed at work and hurt my coworker.\n\nI keep thinking it hasn’t been that long, but then I remember we had that delivery schedule change the week after, and that was already two weeks ago, so… yeah. About three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. I still try to do them, like watching my shows after work, sitting with my husband, but it’s like I’m not really getting anything from it.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing about it is different — same couch, same TV, he’s right there — and I keep thinking I should feel comfortable, or happy, or at least settled. But I don’t. It’s just kind of blank. Honestly, that’s part of why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t weighed myself or anything.

My jeans feel about the same, I think. I mean, sometimes after work my clothes feel weird anyway because I’m sweaty and dusty and the waistband digs in, so it’s hard to tell. I haven’t had anyone say I look thinner or anything like that.

So… no, not that I’ve noticed.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. And I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this should feel nice, I should feel comfortable or happy or something.\n\nBut it’s just… blank. Like the warm part of me got switched off. Even when nothing is wrong in that exact moment, I can’t seem to feel the good part of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're worthless or that you're a burden to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say “worthless,” exactly. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? I mean, I do worry I’m not pulling my weight sometimes, like at home, but then I also think everybody feels that way when they’re worn down.\n\nMy husband doesn’t say I’m a burden or anything. But no, I don’t think I’ve been sitting there telling myself I’m worthless. Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. I think I’m just… tired of trying to guess when it will.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. Since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of feeling noticeably worse than your current state over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “periods,” exactly. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? I guess I haven’t really noticed a clear stretch where I was way worse than I am now. It’s more… up and down, but I couldn’t tell you in a neat way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I try to. Like, I’ll put on my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to, and I’m just sitting there staring at the screen. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is I just don’t feel like myself. Like the warm part of me shut off or something. I’ll sit with my husband, or I’ll try to watch one of my shows after work—you know, the kind I usually like, where I’d normally relax a little—and I know I should feel comfort, or even just a little bit happy, but there’s just… nothing there.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m just tired from work, because stocking all day, it wears you out, and people don’t realize how much lifting and walking it is—but it feels different than that. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not feeling the good parts. That scared me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I try to do the normal things—sit with my husband, put on one of my shows after work, the ones I used to actually look forward to. That sounds so harsh, like something you’d say in one of those after-school specials, you know? Not in those words.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how quickly you're able to do things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, work always makes me tired—stocking shelves and moving boxes, it’s not exactly sitting at a desk. Some days I feel like I’m dragging, but then some days everybody’s dragging, you know?\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to get through one of the aisles and kept thinking about how they changed where they keep the seasonal stuff, which is such a dumb thing to get annoyed about, but it throws off the whole routine. So I don’t know if I’m slower, exactly, or if I’m just noticing everything more. I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that things will never get better for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Never” feels like a big word. I mean, I’ll have moments where I think, if this is how it’s going to feel from now on, I don’t know what I’m supposed to do with that.\n\nBut then I’ll be at the store and some customer asks where the paper towels are, and for a second I’m just answering a normal question, you know? Like life is still doing its thing. So I don’t know if I really believe it’ll never get better. It kind of started after that pallet collapsed at work and my coworker got hurt.\n\nI keep thinking it should’ve just been one of those awful days and then you go back to normal, like when the freezer breaks and everyone’s scrambling and then by next week it’s just a story people tell. But it hasn’t really felt like that. So… yeah, maybe three weeks. It’s not like I marked it on a calendar or anything.\n\nSome days just feel heavier than others, but then that happens depending on the shift too—like if we get a late truck or someone calls out, the whole day feels worse, and then I’m thinking, well, is that me or is that just the day being awful? It feels like the warm part of me just shut off. Like I’m doing the same things, but there’s nothing there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days over the past three weeks where you felt relatively normal or okay?\n\nPatient: Not really normal, no. I mean, there might be a day where I get through a shift and I think, okay, that wasn’t as bad, but then I’ll remember it’s only been like… three weeks since the pallet thing happened, since my coworker got hurt, and it’s like everything comes back to that.\n\nIt’s hard to say “okay,” because I still go to work and do the laundry and all that, but I don’t think I’ve had a day that felt like before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently sad or empty most of the day, nearly every day, over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: Sad, I don’t know if sad is exactly the word all day. It’s more like… empty, I guess. Like I’ll come home and put on one of my shows, the ones I used to like after work, and my husband’s sitting right there next to me, and I know I’m supposed to feel comforted or happy or just normal, but there’s nothing.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. And I still turn them on, you know, because that’s what I always did.\n\nBut I don’t really feel pleasure from it. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.\n\nThat’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean… some days I feel low, but then I get distracted by work stuff or what needs to be done at home. Like yesterday I was trying to remember if we were out of laundry detergent, and then I ended up cleaning under the sink for no real reason.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. Not even anything special, you know, just that normal end-of-the-day feeling.\n\nAnd now I’ll still sit there, the TV’s on, he’s there, and I’m thinking about stupid things like whether I forgot to move boxes at work or if the freezer door got left open—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. It’s just… I know I should feel comfort, or a little happy, or something. But there’s just nothing there.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt depressed most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… I don’t know. “Depressed” feels like such a big word, and I keep trying to compare it to what I think depressed is supposed to look like.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and I’m still doing things, still moving boxes, still answering people if they ask where something is. I mean, I haven’t been checking the scale or anything. My jeans feel… about the same, I guess. I did think about weighing myself the other day because my sister has this whole thing with meal planning and she’s always talking about numbers, but I didn’t actually do it.\n\nSo, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and still find pleasure in, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I used to like watching my shows after work, just kind of sitting with my husband and zoning out a little. And I still turn them on, you know, because that’s what I always did.\n\nBut I don’t really feel pleasure from it. It’s like I know I should feel comforted, or even just a tiny bit happy being there with him, and instead there’s just… nothing. Honestly, that’s kind of what scared me enough to come in, like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I haven’t totally stopped, I don’t think. Like, I still put my shows on after work because that’s just what I do, and my husband’s there and everything’s technically normal.\n\nBut it’s like… I’m not really doing it the same way. I’m sitting there, but there’s no comfort in it. No little happy feeling. And then I start thinking about how I used to look forward to that, even if it was just background noise after a long shift, and now it’s just… blank.\n\nThat’s what made me come in, honestly. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean… some days I feel low, but then I get distracted by work stuff or what needs to be done at home. Like yesterday I was trying to remember if we were out of laundry detergent, and then I ended up cleaning under the sink for no real reason.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. It’s hard for me to measure it like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy and now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s the main thing, actually. I used to like watching my shows after work, just sitting there with my husband. Not even anything special, you know, just that normal end-of-the-day feeling.\n\nAnd now I’ll still sit there, the TV’s on, he’s there, and I’m thinking about stupid things like whether I forgot to move boxes at work or if the freezer door got left open—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. It’s just… I know I should feel comfort, or a little happy, or something. But there’s just nothing there.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt depressed most of the day for most days over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… I don’t know. “Depressed” feels like such a big word, and I keep trying to compare it to what I think depressed is supposed to look like.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and I’m still doing things, still moving boxes, still answering people if they ask where something is. And then I get home and I’m tired, but I mean, I’m always tired after a shift, you know? So I don’t know if that counts as depressed most of the day.\n\nI guess I’ve felt off a lot, but saying most of the day for most days… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s been about three weeks now, since that pallet collapsed at work and hurt my coworker.\n\nI keep thinking it hasn’t been that long, but then I remember we had that delivery schedule change the week after, and that was already two weeks ago, so… yeah. About three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. I still try to do them, like watching my shows after work, sitting with my husband, but it’s like I’m not really getting anything from it.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing about it is different — same couch, same TV, he’s right there — and I keep thinking I should feel comfortable, or happy, or at least settled. But I don’t. It’s just kind of blank. Honestly, that’s part of why I came in. It feels like the warm part of me shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past three weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t really know. I haven’t weighed myself or anything.\n\nMy jeans feel about the same, I think. I mean, sometimes after work my clothes feel weird anyway because I’m sweaty and dusty and the waistband digs in, so it’s hard to tell. I haven’t had anyone say I look thinner or anything like that.\n\nSo… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… yeah, I think so. I mean, it kind of depends on the day, but honestly it’s been there pretty much every day since that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt.\n\nThat was about three weeks ago now. I keep counting it by work stuff, like what shifts I had and which deliveries came in, because the days all sort of blur together. But yes… it’s been going on around three weeks."
